Sex reads 'Female' by construction, which is precisely why it is removed
from the candidate set inside its own stratum (decision 33).
Outcome: days_absent_total (days, capped at 90 in Ellis)

                 Stratum      n Mean days (wtd) Variance (wtd) Var / mean
                  Female 24,273           1.532          18.40      12.02
                    Male 24,659           1.075          13.95      12.97
 Pooled (Table 3 sample) 48,932           1.281          16.02      12.50
 Zero days Max
     64.5%  62
     74.1%  63
     69.3%  63

Equidispersion would put variance / mean at 1. Neither stratum is close, so
stage two is negative binomial in both, exactly as in model-1 and model-2.
The pooled row is there for one purpose: to show that stratifying does not
change the shape of the outcome, only its level. Whatever differs between
the two models will be a difference in association, not in likelihood.

</table></div>Output out1: What stratification does to the candidate set

Section 5.9 asks for 2 models. The PUMF supports 2.
Section 5.4 describes 4 specifications, all of which are carried (decision 34),
so model-3 estimates 8 negative binomial models from 4 cross-validated penalised fits.

Sex leaves the candidate set and becomes the stratification:

  In model-1 (pooled), sex survived the penalty in these specifications:
 spec_id columns retained
 model_3                1
 model_4                1

  That coefficient is a single multiplicative shift applied to every
  respondent. Stratification replaces it with two complete models, in
  which every coefficient is free to differ. The two are answers to
  different questions and neither supersedes the other.

Candidate counts offered inside a stratum:

              Predictor set Section 5.4 count Offered in a stratum Removed
 excluding perceived health                45                   44     sex
 including perceived health                48                   47     sex

No other candidate is dropped a priori. Table t2 records any variable that
lost all but one level inside a stratum; at this sample size there should
be none, and a non-zero count there is worth investigating.
Output out2: Sample construction arithmetic

Source rows (Ellis analytic table):      63,843 
Analytic frame, complete on 48:          48,932  (76.64%)
  Female stratum:                        24,273  (49.61% of the frame)
  Male stratum:                          24,659  (50.39% of the frame)
  Female + Male:                         48,932 

The analytic frame is the model-1 sample, unchanged (decision 32). The two
strata partition it: every respondent appears in exactly one, and the sum
of the two is the whole. This is the structural difference from model-2,
whose seventeen subgroups overlapped heavily and summed to far more than
the frame. Nothing here needs a comorbidity correction.

What replaces it is imbalance. Design columns exceeding the conventional
negligible-difference threshold of 0.1 in absolute standardised difference:

 design columns compared |SMD| > 0.10 |SMD| > 0.25 largest |SMD|
                      96           28            9         0.733
<div style="border: 1px solid #ddd; padding: 5px; overflow-x: scroll; width:100%; "><table class="table table-striped table-hover table-condensed" style="width: auto !important; margin-left: auto; margin-right: auto;">
<caption>Table t21: Weighted prevalence of each chronic condition within each sex stratum, ordered by the size of the gap. The exposures the study is about are themselves unequally distributed between the strata. A condition retained in the female model and not the male one may be more prevalent, more strongly associated with absence, or simply better powered among women; this table settles the first of those three and the other two remain open.</caption>
 <thead>
  <tr>
   <th style="text-align:left;"> Condition </th>
   <th style="text-align:right;"> n (F) </th>
   <th style="text-align:right;"> Prevalence (F) </th>
   <th style="text-align:right;"> n (M) </th>

<caption>Table t3: Decision registry for model-3, decisions 31 through 40. Numbering continues from model-2 so that a decision id is unambiguous across the project.</caption>
 <thead>
  <tr>
   <th style="text-align:right;"> # </th>
   <th style="text-align:left;"> Decision </th>
   <th style="text-align:left;"> Alternatives considered </th>
   <th style="text-align:left;"> Chosen </th>
   <th style="text-align:left;"> Rationale </th>
   <th style="text-align:left;"> Reverse by editing </th>
  </tr>
 </thead>
<tbody>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 31 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> The stratification variable </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> sex as recorded in DHH_SEX; a gender variable; sex crossed with age or cycle </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> sex (DHH_SEX), two levels </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Section 5.9 says 'female respondents' and 'male respondents'. The PUMF carries DHH_SEX, a binary item, in both cycles, and neither cycle carries a gender-identity question. The report therefore says sex and not gender throughout, and the two-level structure is a property of the instrument rather than a modelling choice. No respondent is excluded by the stratification: DHH_SEX has no missing values in the analytic frame. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `stratum_var` and `strata_levels` in declare-globals </td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 32 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Stratum base sample </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Re-derive complete cases inside each stratum; filter the model-1 complete-case frame </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Filter the model-1 frame </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Re-deriving would give each stratum its own denominator and its own candidate set, so the female and male models would not be comparable with each other and neither would be comparable with Table 3. Filtering makes each stratum a strict subset of the Section 5.5 analytic sample, and makes the two strata a partition of it. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `ds1` in tweak-data-0 </td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 33 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Candidate set inside a stratum </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Keep sex in the candidate set; remove it; remove it and add its interactions </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Remove sex; keep everything else that is informative </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Sex is constant inside its own stratum by construction and carries no information there. Nothing else is removed a priori: a variable is dropped only if it loses all but one level inside the stratum, and every such drop is recorded in Table t2. Interactions are not introduced, because stratification IS the interaction Section 5.9 asks for - fitting the model separately by sex allows every coefficient to differ, which a single sex-by-term interaction set would only do for the terms chosen to carry it. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `stratum_candidates()` in declare-functions </td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 34 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> How many Section 5.4 specifications are carried </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> One (the Section 5.5 primary); two (one predictor set at both penalties, as model-2 does); all four </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> All four </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Section 5.9 refers selection back to Section 5.4, which describes four specifications on two axes. model-2 carried only the primary predictor set because a condition subgroup of a few hundred respondents cannot support a four-way comparison. A sex stratum is roughly half the Section 5.5 sample, so that constraint does not apply, and carrying all four makes Table 5 comparable with Table 3 specification by specification. Models 1 and 3 share one cross-validated fit and Models 2 and 4 share another, so four specifications cost two fits per stratum, not four. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `model_specs` and `candidate_sets` in declare-globals </td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 35 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Penalty rule designated primary </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> lambda.1se as Section 5.5 designates; lambda.min as model-2 designates for small subgroups </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> lambda.1se, without perceived health (Model 1) </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> model-2 reversed this ordering with decision 25, but the argument there was scale: a parsimonious penalty applied to a subgroup of a few hundred respondents frequently returns nothing at all. In model-1 the 1SE rule retained 10 variables at n = 48,932, and a sex stratum is about half of that, so the rule has something to say. Section 5.5's designation therefore stands unamended. Table t5 reports what each rule retained in each stratum so the choice remains checkable. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `primary_spec` in declare-globals </td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 36 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Design effect basis </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Reuse model-1's full-sample Kish design effect; compute one within each stratum </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Within each stratum </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> A design effect describes the weight distribution of the sample whose intervals it widens. Weights inside a sex stratum are not distributed as they are in the pooled sample, and Output out5 shows the realised value differing between the two. This repeats model-2's decision 29 rather than inheriting it, so that the two reports can diverge without either breaking silently. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `build_stratum()` in declare-functions </td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 37 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Weight rescaling </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Carry model-1's weight into the stratum unchanged; rescale to mean 1 within each stratum </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Rescale within each stratum </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> glm.nb treats prior weights as frequencies when estimating dispersion, so a stratum whose carried-forward weights average far from 1 would return a theta on a different scale from the other. Rescaling inside the stratum makes theta comparable between the female and male models. Point estimates are unaffected; only the scale of the weights changes. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `build_stratum()` in declare-functions </td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 38 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Section 5.10 resampling method </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> 5-fold cross-validation; leave-one-out; a single train / test split </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> 5-fold, in both variants of inherited decision 16 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Section 5.10 names five folds and, unlike Section 5.8, does not offer leave-one-out - which is consistent, because both strata are large. The term set is scored twice over: once held fixed at what the whole stratum selected (Table t63, the literal reading), and once with the entire two-stage procedure re-run inside each training fold (Table t64, the honest estimate). Folds that fail to converge are counted, not silently dropped. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `perf_folds` in declare-globals </td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 39 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Basis for the female-male comparison </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Compare the two selected models term by term; compare only terms selected in both; refit both strata on the union of the two selected sets </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Refit on the union set </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Section 5.9 asks the stratified models to 'highlight potential differences in the determinants of absenteeism between men and women'. Comparing the two SELECTED models cannot do that cleanly: a term missing from the male model may be absent because the association is absent, or because a penalty declined to select it, and the two are not distinguishable from the output. Refitting both strata, without penalty, on the union of the terms either stratum retained under the primary specification gives every term an estimate on both sides. Selection asymmetry is then reported separately, as a fact about the procedure, in the 'Retained in' column of Table t65. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `union_terms` and `nb_union` in the t65 block </td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
   <td style="text-align:right;"> 40 </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Rare levels and unstable terms </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Collapse rare levels; drop variables that have a rare level; retain and flag </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> Retain and flag at SE &gt; 2 on the log scale </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> The same device model-2 adopted with decision 27, re-made here rather than inherited. It matters much less at stratum scale than at subgroup scale - both strata carry more than twenty thousand respondents - but a factor level that is rare in the population is rare in both strata, and a flagged term is cheaper than a silently uninformative interval. </td>
   <td style="text-align:left;"> `unstable_se` in declare-globals </td>
  </tr>
</tbody>

</table></div>Output out5: Variance-estimation instruments for Section 5.9

Section 5.9 asks for 500 bootstrap replicate weights, the same instrument
Sections 5.3, 5.5 and 5.7 ask for. They do not exist in the 2010 or 2014
PUMF (inherited decision 10), and strata and PSU identifiers are
suppressed, so no fully design-based variance estimator can be built for
either stratum. What follows are two LOWER bounds on how far the
model-based intervals understate uncertainty. Neither sees clustering.

Kish design effect, computed WITHIN each stratum (decision 36):

 Stratum      n Kish deff Effective n SE multiplier
  Female 24,273     3.742       6,487         1.934
    Male 24,659     3.518       7,010         1.876

Huber-White sandwich SE multiplier, over every term in every fitted model:

 Stratum Terms Median Minimum Maximum
  Female   101  1.743   0.567   4.762
    Male   138  1.777   0.565   4.192

Both instruments are close to their model-1 values, which is what a
stratification of a large sample should produce: halving a sample does not
change how unequal its weights are. Table t62 reports all three interval
variants side by side and marks terms whose standard error exceeds 2
on the log scale (decision 40).
<div style="border: 1px solid #ddd; padding: 0px; overflow-y: scroll; height:700px; overflow-x: scroll; width:100%; "><table class="table table-striped table-hover table-condensed" style="width: auto !important; margin-left: auto; margin-right: auto;">
<caption>Table t6 (deliverable Table 5): Adjusted prevalence rate ratios with 95% Wald intervals for every term retained inside each sex stratum, under all four Section 5.4 specifications. An asterisk marks Wald p &lt; 0.05; an exclamation mark flags a term whose standard error exceeds 2 on the log scale (decision 40). A blank cell means the term was not retained by that specification. These are NOT bootstrap intervals (inherited decision 10), and they ignore the selection step that produced the term list. Table t62 carries the log-scale coefficients, standard errors, exact p-values and two wider interval variants; Table t65 carries the female-male comparison, which this table deliberately does not support.</caption>

<caption>Table t65: The female-male comparison Section 5.9 asks for. Both strata are refitted without penalty on the union of the terms either retained under the primary specification (decision 39), so every term has an estimate on both sides and 'not selected' is never mistaken for 'null'. 'Retained in' records what the penalty did; the estimates record what the data say. The ratio of rate ratios is the multiplicative difference between the female and male associations, and its interval and z-test use se = sqrt(se_F^2 + se_M^2), which is valid because the two strata are disjoint samples. Rows are ordered by p; the table is a screen for heterogeneity, NOT a set of confirmatory tests - it carries 10 comparisons with no multiplicity control, and all of them inherit the selection step that produced the union set.</caption>

Read them down a stratum, never across. The held-out versions, which are the ones Section 5.10 actually asks for, are in Tables t63 and t64.</caption>

 Stratum models converged failed
  Female      4         4      0
    Male      4         4      0

No sex-stratified model failed to converge.

No specification returned an empty selection in either stratum. This is
the contrast with model-2 that justifies decision 35: at stratum scale
the parsimonious rule still has something to say, so Section 5.5's
designation of it as primary needs no amendment.

Dispersion (theta; smaller means more overdispersed):

 Stratum Specification  Theta
  Female       Model 1 0.2217
  Female       Model 2 0.2372
  Female       Model 3 0.2401
  Female       Model 4 0.2546
    Male       Model 1 0.1510
    Male       Model 2 0.1614
    Male       Model 3 0.1624
    Male       Model 4 0.1702

Theta is comparable between the strata because the weights are rescaled to
mean 1 inside each of them (decision 37). A lower theta in one stratum
means the count is more overdispersed there once the model has had its say.

</table></div>Output out6: Reading the C-statistic, and the size of the selection optimism

Share of respondents at zero days, by stratum:

 Stratum Zero days
  Female     64.5%
    Male     74.1%

Harrell's C counts a pair only when the two respondents differ on the
outcome. Most respondents in both strata report zero days, so most pairs
are incomparable and never enter the denominator. Table t63 reports the
comparable share beside every C for exactly this reason, and the female
and male C-statistics should be compared only alongside those shares.

Selection optimism (fixed minus re-run), primary specification:

 Stratum      n  d RMSE d McFadden     d C
  Female 24,273 +0.0211    +0.0076 +0.0274
    Male 24,659 -0.4970    +0.0007 -0.0005

A positive d McFadden or d C is the amount by which evaluating a model on
data that helped select it flatters that model. model-1 found this quantity
to be essentially zero at n = 48,932; a stratum is about half that, so the
same reassurance should be earned rather than assumed.

Term-set instability under refitting, all four specifications:

Both columns count DESIGN COLUMNS, not variables, so that the full-stratum
fit and the fold refits are measured on the same scale.

 Stratum Specification Full-stratum columns Across folds (mean)
  Female       Model 1                    9                10.2
  Female       Model 2                   18                18.8
  Female       Model 3                   71                65.8
  Female       Model 4                   82                80.6
    Male       Model 1                    8                 9.4
    Male       Model 2                   16                16.2
    Male       Model 3                   57                52.6
    Male       Model 4                   53                45.2
 Across folds (range)
                 4-27
                13-39
                57-78
                75-87
                 8-12
                15-18
                40-65
                31-61

  cross-validation runtime: 4.7 minutes

Proposal sections model-3 closes:
  - Section 5.9 sex-stratified multivariable models (Table 5 = t6 / t62)
  - Section 5.10 model performance evaluation (t61 in-sample, t63 / t64 held out)

Requirements NOT met, and why:
  - Bootstrap variance estimation (inherited decision 10: replicate weights
    absent from the PUMF). Sandwich and Kish-inflated intervals stand in as
    lower bounds, exactly as in model-00, model-1 and model-2.
  - Section 5.1 Little's MCAR test and multiple imputation are not performed.
  - The female-male comparison of Table t65 is a screen, not a confirmatory
    test: no multiplicity control, and every row inherits the selection step
    that produced the union term set (decision 39).

With model-3, every modelling section of the proposal has an estimation of
record: 5.3 in model-00, 5.4 to 5.6 in model-1, 5.7 to 5.8 in model-2, and
5.9 to 5.10 here. Downstream reports should read the artifacts in Table t7
rather than re-estimate.
